SOCIAL ENCOUNTERS
This chapter provides the Referee with tools to generate
useful encounters with NPCs in a variety of social
contexts. The intent is to assist the Referee in developing
a sense of verisimilitude in their campaign world and
increase variety in NPC interac�ons with the player
characters. We divide this chapter into two main parts:
the first part is a series of encounter tables for a variety
of contexts, while the second consists of tables and
generators to help Referees make their NPCs more
useful, mo�vated, and colorful.
ENCOUNTER TYPES
There are 6 basic types of encounters: Rou�ne, Legal,
Random, Patron, and Animal.
Rou�ne Encounters: these represent ordinary day-to-
day encounters with people performing their rou�ne
```
jobs: technicians in starports, clerks in stores, and
```
bartenders in saloons. The Referee should not be overly
concerned with checking for rou�ne encounters, as
common sense will dictate whether or not a starport bar
```
will have a bartender and a server or two (it will).
```
Instead, the Referee should use the NPC Reac�on Table
as well as the tables presented below to provide color to
any rou�ne encounter.
Legal Encounters: these are a specific kind of random
encounter where one or more player characters interact
with law enforcement officials. Depending on what the
PCs are up to, the society’s government type, and the
NPC Reac�on Roll result, legal encounters can run the
gamut from minor inconveniences, downright
harrowing, to the full evening’s adventure if things go
horribly wrong. The higher the world’s Law Level, the
more characters can expect to be bothered by local law
enforcement.
The Referee should throw the local Law Level or greater
once per day to avoid an encounter with local law
enforcement.
Random Encounters: there are two main benefits to
incorpora�ng random encounters into any gaming
session. The first is that it is possible to use random
encounters to dynamically generate an evening’s
adventure. This is especially useful when the Referee
knows where the player characters are intending to do—
by asking what the players intend to do in the next
session—and preparing random encounters
beforehand. This may seem somewhat disingenuous.
A�er all, aren’t random encounters supposed to occur
randomly during play? This is possible, but it means that
the Referee will be forced to work on the fly to generate
a random encounter. Usually, this limits the number of
random encounters the PCs will interact with to a
maximum of one. This works against the second
benefit of incorpora�ng randomly generated
encounters, which is to establish a sense of
```
verisimilitude: the illusion that the game world
```
exists beyond the characters’ experiences and
ac�ons. If players know they will have one random
encounter in an evening, they may not even wish
to interact with the randomly generated NPC.
However, if they encounter a fully formed
randomly generated NPC, they will be much more
interested in engaging with the character.
The Referee should roll 1D per day. A 5 or 6
indicates that a random encounter occurs.
Referees can use the tables in this chapter to
determine what sort of NPC is encountered, or
they can develop their own random encounter
tables for specific se�ngs. We have provided a
few sample random encounter tables to choose
from.
Patron Encounters: patrons are those valuable
NPCs that provide tools, money, or other support
to player characters. Referees are encouraged to
use patrons as a means to engage PCs in
adventures. Typically, the patron represents the
hook that is the basis of the adventure and
provides the reward at the end of it—if the player
characters were successful, of course.
The Referee should roll 1D once per week. On a 5
or 6, the player characters have randomly
encountered a patron. Referees should feel free to
use the NPC genera�on tables below to develop
the patron further. Make this roll in addi�on to
whenever player characters use skills like Liason or
```
Carouse (among others) to locate patrons
```
themselves.
Animal Encounters: rules for these are in the
Xenofauna Encounters chapter.

PATRON TABLES
Patrons are usually central figures in a Referee’s
adventure. It is important to make them unique, so that
the players easily remember them. If possible, Referees

D6 Trustworthiness

3 They’ll hold up their end of the deal, but only if forced to.
4 They’ll pay without any problems.
5 They’ll pay more than was originally bargained for.
6 They’ll pay generously and will give bonuses for good performances.
D6 Why did they hire the PCs

193
Many worlds bear life, and this creates ecosystems, food
chains, and the creatures that populate them.
Interstellar visitors entering such alien ecosystems
encounter xenofauna – alien animals. The la�er respond
in a manner dictated by their ins�ncts, from flight, to
indifference, to trying to eat the hapless adventurer.
```
Alien beasts vary greatly; however, basic ecological
```
func�ons show great similarity between worlds. There
are four basic categories of animals, based on diet:
herbivore, omnivore, carnivore, and scavenger. Each
category has sub-categories depending on how the
animal gets its food. The following animal genera�on
system creates alien animals based on their ecological
func�on, rather than their form. The animal’s exact form
is up to the referee.
We encourage the referee to be crea�ve and modify
animals they might already know about, as they see fit.
For example, poison and venom are common in the
natural world here on Terra and would most likely be
```
common on other worlds; the referee may give an
```
animal a venom based on the poison rules later in this
chapter. More radical varia�ons of known creatures are
also possible and encouraged. For example, the referee
may devise a predator hun�ng by spi�ng thorns at its
prey at high speed. In this case, they may decide that the
animal’s a�ack will resemble a rifle. Psionic animals are
also a possibility. Do not let the tables restrict you – they
are merely tools to provide a baseline.
ANIMAL GENERATION PROCESS
Follow these steps to generate xenofauna.
1. Choose the animal’s habitat terrain and roll for
its movement type.
2. Determine the animal’s ecological niche and
subtype.
3. Determine size, stamina, lifeblood, and damage.
4. Determine weapons, armor, and base speed.
TERRAIN AND ECOSYSTEM
Terrain and the local ecosystem affect the types of
creatures inhabi�ng them. Use the following table
to determine the animal’s mode of locomo�on.
The result provides DMs for later tables based on
terrain. Roll 1D and consult this table for the
animal’s method of movement: A for Amphibious,
F for Flying, S for Swimming, and W for Walking.
The numbers following the movement type are
further DMs to the animal’s size.

ANIMAL SIZE, STAMINA, LIFEBLOOD,
AND DAMAGE
For each creature, throw 2D for size, applying any size
DMs from the Terrain and Movement table. Size
determines the creature’s Lifeblood, Stamina, and
damage. As for sen�ent beings, Stamina and Lifeblood
indicate how much damage the animal can take. Throw
for two numbers as indicated on the table. The first
```
number is Stamina; the second is Lifeblood. The damage
```
column modifies the amount of damage dice the
animal’s a�acks inflict. See the animal weapons
table for the baseline damage. For example, if a
weapon causes 2D damage by default but the
“Damage Modifier” column indicated -1D, this
animal’s weapon will only cause 1D damage. The
entry “x2”, on the other hand, will modify the
damage to 6D. If the table reduces a weapon’s
damage dice to zero, and each a�ack causes only
1 point of damage.

XENOFAUNA ENCOUNTER EXAMPLE
Isaac would like to generate a xenofauna encounter for
a group of player characters travelling in the mountain

1. Choose the animal’s habitat terrain and roll for its
movement type.
The characters are hiking in the mountains looking for an

result F-6. This is a flying creature with a size modifier of
DM-6.
2. Determine the animal’s ecological niche and
subtype.

Herbivore. He knows what a herbivore is. Isaac rolls 2D
```

```

ea�ng. Like a hummingbird, giant insect, or even
waterfowl.
3. Determine size, stamina, lifeblood, and damage.

Size 6 has no modifiers for creature weapon damage.
4. Determine weapons, armor, and base speed.
```

```

```
table, applying a DM-6 for herbivores: 6 – 6 (Herbivore
```
```
DM) = 0. This means that the creature only has its
```
“hooves” that do 2D damage. This creature is some sort
of flying grazer that lives in the mountains and has no
offensive weapons.
```

```
- 12 (Number rolled on the size table DM) +4 (Herbivore)
```
– 2 (Flyers) = 14. This gives the creature a natural
```

REFEREE ADVICE
This chapter provides advice on various subjects related
to Refereeing a game of Cepheus Deluxe.
BUILDING A CEPHEUS DELUXE SETTING
The world genera�on chapter provides the Referee with
the tools to develop the worlds and poli�cs in a
subsector of space. However, before adventuring can
begin, Referees should develop some underlying
assump�ons about their game world, and the kind of
game they are running. Not everything has to be
decided immediately before gameplay begins—the
players will no doubt inadvertently offer their own ideas
for the se�ng, and the Referee should eagerly adopt
them—but there are all sorts of ques�ons you should
ask yourself as the Referee at some point before the
players are at the table, and the dice are rolled.
Think about the theme and tone of the game:
Swashbuckling space opera games play very differently
than games about desperate wars against bizarre aliens.
What sort of campaign are you interested in running?
Restless space-truckers hauling freight and ge�ng into
weird situa�ons is a standard mode of play, but it has its
own assump�ons about se�ng, themes, and tone of
play baked in. What sorts of adventures should the
Referee develop? Who are the recurring villains, friends,
and patrons? What are the big and li�le problems in the
se�ng that require a group of self-interested player
characters to sort out in some way?
If you as a Referee don’t have the answers to these
ques�ons immediately, that’s alright. Start small, see
how the players react to the situa�ons they find
themselves in and the obstacles you place in their path.
Then, as you get a sense of what is interes�ng to them
individually and as a whole, you can grow your campaign
world accordingly. Remember: the campaign se�ng
exists as a pla�orm for player character adventuring and
interac�on. Trying to run a different game than the one
your players are currently playing in will rapidly become
an exercise in frustra�on. Conserve your energy and give
your players what they want—while s�ll making them
work hard for everything.
Use the World Genera�on system, but do not adhere to
the dice throws in all cases. Adjust sta�s�cs to produce
the loca�ons that you want. But do not ignore the dice.
Allow for some loca�ons that are strange, bizarre, and
surprising to the players. Science fic�on is the place for
the new, unusual, and unique journeys.
At the outset, one planet at the center of the map, with
a few worlds at Jump-1, 2 or 3 away will be enough to
start adventuring. The central planet should be
sufficiently well-se�led to equip the PCs for their first
adventures. It is a borderland at the edge of “proper
civiliza�on” before the fron�er begins. Civiliza�on
```
is in one direc�on; the unknown is in the other.
```
Add in more worlds as the PCs move from world to
world.
Think about the organiza�on of inhabited worlds.
Are the worlds all under one interstellar
government, or are there smaller compe�ng
governments with no more than a few worlds
under their control? Some planets may be
independent and outside the reach or at the edge
of a larger government. What does that mean for
visitors?
Not every element in the se�ng needs some level
of explana�on for why it is there but having a
general sense of the se�ng’s history will help
explain things on the fly. Some worlds are the core
of their culture and government, usually with
higher popula�on and technology. Others will be
fron�er colonies, with few people and less
technology. In between are worlds with be�er or
worse development. The age of your se�ng will
force you to ask many ques�ons and provide many
```
answers: why are there humans living on the
```
fron�er in the first place? Was it once a highly
populated part of space? What was the calamity
that tore the old civiliza�on apart? Are there
remnants and reminders of the ancient �mes
sca�ered everywhere? Or is this truly the high
fron�er, where no human has ventured before?
Whenever possible, use some aspect of a world's
profile to remind players that they are not on
Earth. Different gravity, air without oxygen, radical
topography, odd-colored skies, these are all
reminders of being in another place. Humans
prefer certain world characteris�cs. Many world
will be reasonably Earth-like, and it is easy enough
to understand why people se�led there. Yet there
must be a good reason for living on say, a heavy-
gravity world with an acidic atmosphere. These
kinds of worlds can exist too: all that is needed is a
good reason to be there. That reason might also
be why the PCs venture there. Empty planets on
the map may not really be empty, it’s just that no
one has discovered the reason for humans to
inhabit them yet.
Add elements to the se�ng map as you need
them. Trying to map out and describe a dozen
worlds all at once before the campaign has even
begun can be overwhelming. The players won't
visit all your worlds in the first adventure—and
some might never be visited—so add the details of
the loca�on when the PCs arrive. If the players
never head to a place you’ve prepared, file off the
names, and reuse your hard work. They will never
know the difference.

HIGH LEVEL PLAY IN CEPHEUS DELUXE
For a game experience different from roaming around
the galaxy figh�ng random villains, Referees and players
might try se�ng out for High Level Play. This mode of
campaigning is not represented mechanically on the
```
character sheet; it exists in the game se�ng as the PCs
```
gain power and influence over the se�ng itself. You’ll
know when High Level Play is happening when the
regional governor or planetary dictator has to take the
PC's reac�on to his plans into account.
How does High Level Play start? Say a PC musters out
with a merchant ship. The player could decide that one
ship is not enough: he wants a whole fleet of them! An
army veteran might aim to build a private army of 10000
soldiers and vehicles. Then she can overthrow the
oppressive government of the dictator under whom she
served. A Rogue might try the same, but by means of
sabotage, espionage, and poli�cal maneuvers. Scouts
might claim ownership of a newly-discovered planet,
rich in valuable resources. Elites could become the
power behind the throne, or even the Power ON the
throne.
High Level Play o�en involves amassing:
• Money and sources of income
• Weapons and people to use them
• Ships and crews
• Social Standing
• A Cause to pursue.
Money can be made through interstellar trade, by
land ownership, found treasures, or the�.
Weapons, ships, and hirelings are available to hire
by people with money. Social Standing can be
advanced by means of lavish spending, or by
interac�ng with NPCs that already have it. Rulers
can award �tles and status to PCs for services
rendered and public goods achieved, or for a large
enough cash dona�on.
The Cause can be any goal that the players can
devise. The players should share this goal with the
Referee. The Referee is not obligated to help the
```
players to accomplish it; the Referee's job is to
```
provide meaningful obstacles and challenges as
the players work towards the goal. There should
be no guarantee that the players will ever achieve
their goal, though it should certainly be feasible
given the right decisions and a li�le luck. Clever
planning and bold execu�on are the key!
There are no levels or ranks in High Level Play, only
comparisons between powerful en��es and
groups. The Referee should be tracking which
NPCs have more money, more followers, and more
ships than the Pcs. However, High Level Play
should not devolve into Adventures in Accoun�ng.
If the PCs use hirelings with Administra�on skill,
the bookkeeping can be pushed off to them.
Adventures, Crises and Making History should be
the main focus of any High Level Play campaign.

SUPER-SCIENCE
The equipment list in Cepheus Deluxe is extensive but
makes no claims at completeness. This is inten�onal, as
it allows the players and referee to borrow materials
from elsewhere, and to further imagine and invent new
technologies for the se�ng. These new technologies can
be invented, found, bought, or stolen.
As long as they do not disrupt the game se�ng, the
Referee should allow most tech ideas. However, that
does not mean that the PCs should easily acquire new
technology. They should have to work for it, and that
means adventures to find parts, schema�cs, and the
know-how to put something together. It could also mean
prying new technology free from the cold, dead,
appendages of whoever was its previous owner…
However, it is important to note some hard limits on
new technology. The Cepheus Deluxe game has a few
technological assump�ons baked in. These should never
be considered hard limita�ons but know that changing
these assump�ons will result in a very, very different
style of science fic�on. Among these assump�ons are:
• No FTL communica�on
• Slow interstellar and interplanetary travel
• A Preference for physical rather than virtual ac�vity
• Physical weapons are more common than energy
weapons
• Human-centric ac�on
Before introducing a new technology, the Referee
should consider how the device relates to those
defining factors. Avoid se�ng-breaking
```
technologies; an FTL radio would radically change
```
the se�ng as given. Further ques�ons to ask:
• Will it make the PCs too powerful, too fast?
• Why isn't this standard already, or should it be
standard?
• Does it duplicate already exis�ng technology?
• How will governments and socie�es respond
```
to this; will it be commonly legal or illegal?
```
• How much should it cost to purchase or
repair?
• At what Tech Level does it become available?
• In what ways could the PCs abuse this device?
• Will use of this device subs�tute for a PC
```
having a skill? (Do not allow these)
```
If a new device is powerful, it must have
limita�ons. Law Level and price are effec�ve
means to curb the availability and use of such
items. Referees should remember that if it is not
unique, NPCs may have the device as well.
Acquiring a unique item should be a major
undertaking that spans mul�ple adventures.
However, once the Referee approves the device,
the players can innovate the ways it can be used as
much as they like.

HOW TO USE CONTACTS
Contacts are one of the most useful player and Referee
tools in the game. They provide adventure hooks,
addi�onal resources, and ample fun roleplaying
opportuni�es. Remember that most player characters
will start the game with at least one contact, and they
should be recurring characters for many adventures.
Referees can use contacts as patrons, sources of
informa�on and equipment, or even as the object of a
specific adventure. Patron contacts should be friendly
and trustworthy and should only betray the player
characters with ample forewarning and a possible,
though very costly, way out. These are the PCs’ friends
and colleagues from the old days, a�er all. Contacts will
happily furnish PCs with informa�on and equipment,
but usually will need something in trade: a favor, cash,
equipment, or informa�on. Again, contacts might be
unsavory, but they should be fundamentally useful and
trustworthy. Finally, a contact—especially a par�cularly
beloved contact—can be used as the object of the
adventure itself: perhaps the contact is in trouble
and needs the PCs to get out of a jam. Or they are
unable to take advantage of an opportunity alone
and have called in their old friends to help out, for
a share in the reward. Naturally, a contact can be
abducted, or hurt, or even killed, and the PCs can
set out on a mission to right the wrong. If they lose
their contact in this way, be sure to provide them
with a new contact who is suitably impressed by
the loyalty they have for their old friend.
Keep a record of significant NPCs that the PCs have
encountered, and where they can be found. When
the PCs need help, they should reach out to their
Contacts. The Referee should make note of
Contact reac�ons – strong posi�ve reac�ons could
mean the NPC will help above and beyond what is
asked of them. Strong nega�ve reac�ons could
mean the Contact will betray or abandon the PCs
given the opportunity.

```
(UWP)
```

A UWP is a string of hexadecimal numbers presen�ng
basic informa�on about a world in eight digits.

1. Starport type.
2. World size.
3. Atmosphere.
4. Hydrographics.
5. Popula�on.
6. Government.
7. Law Level.
-dash-
8. Tech level.
For example, the example world of Fros�ang from
the World Genera�on chapter has the following
characteris�cs:
1. Starport E (fron�er starport).
2. World Size 6 (slightly smaller than Terra).
3. Atmosphere Code 1 (Trace)
4. Hydrographics of 7 indicates 70% of the surface
```
is covered by water (in this case – ice).
```
5. Popula�on 3 means 1,000 to 9,999 residents.
6. Government type 1 (Corpora�on)
7. Law Level 1 (minimal law enforcement)
8. Tech level 8 (as given in the Equipment chapter).

BIO RECONSTRUCTION
At Tech Level 16, extremely advanced medical
technology can perform miracles unimaginable by
less advanced socie�es. By applying cu�ng-edge
biotechnology, an advanced hospital or laboratory
can grow back a dead body and restore it to full
func�on. The only requirement for Bio
Reconstruc�on is that the dead character's brain
must be preserved in an intact enough state. This
allows for the scanning of the brain’s neural pathways
for imprin�ng in the new body. Bio Reconstruc�on is
a monstrously expensive process, and it requires an
advanced and full-scale hospital with access to the
best scien�fic facili�es and a large crew of experts in
medicine and biology. Unlike cyborg conversion,
however, this process is far less invasive and has a
be�er chance of success.
The procedure costs MCr60 – as much as a small

BIO RECONSTRUCTION SIDE EFFECTS
These are the various side effects and problems
caused by par�ally successful Bio Reconstruc�ons:
Appearance Change, Radical: The character looks like
a completely new person and is unrecognizable by
anything short of a gene�c test. Even the fingerprints
change. The exact details of this change are up to the
Referee and player.
Appearance Change, Par�al: The character looks
similar to their old self, but not exactly so. People
who know the character may a�empt to recognize
them on a successful INT 6+ throw. Other details and
consequences of this change are up to the Referee
and player.
```
Disfigurement: The character suffers a terrible
```
disfigurement that can only be corrected by a TL13+
hospital at a cost of Cr10,000. It is not debilita�ng but
is ghastly to look at and incurs DM-2 to any skill or
characteris�c throws related to diplomacy or
persuasion. However, the character gains a DM+2 to
any roll related to in�mida�on. The character is also
easily recognizable.
Malignant Illness: Faulty gene�c reconstruc�on
causes a persistent, incurable malignancy in the
character's body. Halve all physical Characteris�cs
and round down to a minimum of 1. Furthermore,
each month the character must throw END 8+ or die
from the illness. Bio reconstruc�on, cyborg
conversion, or any other procedure cannot restore a
character who dies in such a way. This is a good
opportunity to �e up any loose ends from the
character's previous life. They are living on borrowed
�me.
Memory Loss, Full: The character suffers from
complete amnesia, though they retain basic life skills
and par�al adventuring skills. Halve the character's
EDU score, rounding down to a minimum of 1. Also,
for each skill, throw INT 6+ or lose one level of the
```
skill; a skill at level-0 reduced in such manner is lost.
```
Memory Loss, Par�al: The character loses part of
their memory but retains a general idea of their
iden�ty, as well as most skills. The character loses 1d3
points of EDU to a minimum of 1. Randomly choose
one of the character’s skills that are at level-1 or
```
higher: reduce it to level-0.
```
Personality Change: The character retains their
memories. However, their personality has changed
drama�cally and profoundly. The player and Referee
should nego�ate this change. We suggest using some
of the random NPC mo�va�on tables found in the
Social Encounters chapter to guide the process.
Physical Characteris�c Change: The character's new
body grows differently than the last one. Roll 2D
three �mes and assign the values to the three
physical characteris�cs, STR, DEX, and END, as
desired.
Progressive Demen�a: The character's brain was not
properly restored and will deteriorate with �me. Each
month, the character loses 1d3 points of INT and 1d3
points of EDU. When any of these characteris�cs
reaches zero or below, the character falls into a
vegeta�ve state and will die within 1D months. If
both fall below zero on the same month, the
character suffers a stroke and dies immediately.

APPENDIX D: ALIENS
Grand interstellar drama o�en calls for sen�ent
aliens. Unlike the unfathomable alien life of a hard-
science universe, the following three species follow
common space-opera tropes. They are humanoid,
reminiscent of Terran animals, and in one case
inspired by pop-culture UFO lore. These aliens are
close enough to humans to be playable without much
work.
Under these rules, aliens use the same careers as
humans do, but have alien traits that set them apart
from humanity.
For more detailed alien species and cultures, as well
as advanced alien character genera�on, we
recommend our grand space-opera se�ng: These
Stars Are Ours! available from Stellagama Publishing
in PDF and print-on-demand formats.
GRAYS
Short in stature but intellectually powerful, the gray
aliens are a staple of science fic�on and popular
culture. They are commonly depicted flying in
saucers and abduc�ng people for horrific scien�fic
experimenta�on. All grays are biologically female.
They reproduce asexually, each daughter being a
clone of her mother with minimal varia�on. Curiosity
```
drives the grays and they value ra�onality; they are
```
excellent scien�sts and strategists. They are,
however, poor fighters, and prefer to use technology
and the slave races they have subjugated to fight
their ba�les.
Grays have the following traits:
```
• Injured or sick grays require a medical facility (or
```
```
tools) of TL10 or be�er, otherwise they suffer
```
DM-2 to any related medical throws.
• Grays do not need to acclima�ze to low-gravity
environments.
• At character genera�on, grays roll 1D for STR and
1D for END, but 3D for DEX and INT.
REPTILOIDS
These warm-blooded rep�le-like aliens bring the
might of the monitor lizard and the agility of the
gecko to the stars. Males are taller and stronger than
humans, while females are human-sized. Civiliza�on
transformed the male rep�loid’s murderous
territorial ins�ncts into a less destruc�ve, but always
present, compe��ve streak. A male rep�loid lives
and dies for glory - constantly proving his me�le to

the big picture issues important to rep�loid tribal
```
society: government, commerce, and technical
```
development. The males, and to a lesser degree the
females, are excellent warriors, though many try their
```
hand in commerce (and piracy!) as well.
```
Rep�loids have the following traits:
• See twice as far as humans in low-light
condi�ons.
• DM+2 to all Athle�cs throws to climb. May cling

Athle�cs/STR 8+ per round of clinging.
• Rep�loids do no suffer hourly damage from hot

• Males enjoy a +1 bonus to STR and a penalty of
```
-2 to INT; females enjoy a +1 bonus to EDU and a
```
penalty of -2 to STR. This cannot bring INT or STR
to less than 1.
INSECTOIDS
The Insectoids were once the pests living in the
detritus of another more advanced civiliza�on. But
that civiliza�on fell, and the Insectoids soon thrived in
their precursors’ ruins. Opportunis�c and hardy,
these bug-folk quickly learned how to bring their

enabled them to skip many millennia of cultural

trinkets, and boundless opportunists. Terrans may

advantage their trade and diploma�c neutrality.
Insectoids have the following traits:
• Fast-growing. Start their career at the age of 10
and start aging a�er their third term, at the age
of 22. May use career tables for up to 5 terms.
• 2 points of natural armor. Non-cumula�ve with
worn armor heavier than Leather Jacket.
• Insectoids do not need to acclima�ze to low-
gravity environments.
• -2 penalty to EDU. This cannot bring EDU to less
than 1.

